Anchored planar algebras and 3-categorical graphical calculus
Brett Hungar

Abstract
. Anchored planar algebras, a generalized notion of Vaughan Jones’ planar algebras, have recently seen use in higher category theory, functional analysis, and TQFT applications. These algebras are equipped with a natural 3-dimensional graphical calculus. We compare this graphical calculus with the 3-dimensional graphical calculus associated to tricategories, and we show that anchored planar algebras can be thought of as living in a particular tricategory. This allows for more general techniques to be applied to anchored planar algebras and expands the types of diagrams that anchored planar algebras can interpret.



1.
Introduction

Anchored planar algebras were first introduced in
[
HPT16
]
, building off Vaughan Jones’ notion of planar algebras
[
Jon99
]
. These algebras have already seen use in higher category theory and functional analysis as in
[
HPT23
]
, and related graphical calculi have applications in physics
[
HBJP23
,
KJS+24
]
. They are also related to notions of categorified trace, such as the categorified traces discussed in
[
HPT15
]
and
[
PS13
]
. Anchored planar algebras, as algebras over an operad, have a natural graphical calculus arising from that operad. This graphical calculus can be seen to be 3-dimensional by viewing anchored planar tangles as strings on tubes that are allowed to merge and twist. The prototypical example of 3-dimensional graphical calculus comes from 3-categories themselves, viewing objects as regions, 1-cells as sheets, 2-cells as strings, and 3-cells as junctions. It is reasonable to wonder whether there is a connection between these two calculi. In this work, we show that we can interpret the operadic graphical calculus within the graphical calculus of 3-categories.

Theorem A
.

For every planar pivotal 3-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, objects
A,B
, morphisms
M:A\to B
and
M^{*}:B\to A
, and adjoint pair
F:M\boxtimes_{B}M^{*}\to A
,
F^{*}:A\to M\boxtimes_{B}M^{*}
with isomorphisms

\tau^{\ell}:(\operatorname{id}_{M}\boxtimes-)\circ F\to(-^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{M}^{*})\circ F,\ \ \tau^{r}:F^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id}_{M}\boxtimes-)\to F^{*}\circ(-^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{M}^{*}),

satisfying certain axioms, we can construct an anchored planar algebra internal to
\operatorname{End}(1_{A})
which interprets anchored planar tangles via the graphical calculus of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Moreover, all anchored planar algebras in braided fusion categories arise this way.

Theorem B
.

For every anchored planar algebra
{\mathcal{P}}
, there is a 3-category
{\mathcal{C}}
and objects as in Theorem
A
such that the anchored planar algebra internal to the graphical calculus of
{\mathcal{C}}
is equivalent to
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Morally, we should think of this theorem as lending evidence to the idea that all 3D graphical calculi are subexamples of the 3D graphical calculus of a 3-category. In Section
2
, we recall the graphical definition of anchored planar algebras and describe the 3-category in which our construction in Theorem
A
takes place.

Our proof proceeds by taking a pivotal module fusion category for a braided pivotal fusion category and showing that it naturally gives rise to certain maps inside a particular 3-category that define an anchored planar algebra. Moreover, we show that this anchored planar algebra is equivalent to the one given by the construction in
[
HPT16
]
. Since it is already known that every anchored planar algebra arises this way (shown in
[
HPT16
]
), this also shows that every anchored planar algebra can be interpreted internal to the graphical calculus of some 3-category. We discuss this construction and prove Theorem
B
in Section
3
.

Since the graphical calculus of 3-categories can interpret more geometric shapes than the anchored planar operad, this also enriches the algebraic structure of anchored planar algebras. We are able to interpret surfaces of higher genus and comultiplication-like operations, in addition to the generating operations of the anchored planar operad. In Section
3.3
, we use this structure to answer a question from
[
HPT24
, 1.1.3]
.

2.
Background

2.1.
3D graphical calculus of
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}

Our general setting is the 3-category
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
. We begin with a brief discussion of the structure of this 3-category and the various graphical calculi we will use within
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
. Our construction of fusion and braided fusion categories, especially in the enriched setting, is based on
[
EGNO15
]
,
[
JMPP21
]
,
[
BJS21
]
, and
[
KZ18
]
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
BJS21
]
,
[
JMPP21
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a braided fusion category. The 3-category
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
has objects
{\mathcal{A}}
-enriched fusion categories (that is, fusion categories
{\mathcal{X}}
with a braided monoidal functor
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{X}})
); a 1-cell
{}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a (finitely semisimple)
{\mathcal{X}}-{\mathcal{Y}}
bimodule category
{\mathcal{M}}
equipped with an
{\mathcal{A}}
-centered structure; 2-cells are given by bimodule functors; and 3-cells are bimodule natural transformations. Composition of 1-cells is given by relative Deligne product. By a
{\mathcal{A}}
-centered structure on
{}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, we mean a natural isomorphism between the left
{\mathcal{A}}
-action given by
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{X}})
and the right action given by
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{Y}})
. This isomorphism must satisfy appropriate hexagon identities (with the action associators) and must intertwine the tensorators of the
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
actions.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
BJS21
]
,
[
KZ18
]
)
.

Given a braided fusion category
{\mathcal{B}}
and a
{\mathcal{B}}
-enriched fusion category
{\mathcal{Y}}
, there exists a braided fusion category
{\mathcal{A}}
, object
{\mathcal{X}}\in{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
, and 1-cell
{}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{X}}
with monoidal (resp. braided) equivalences
\operatorname{End}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{X}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}}({\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{X}})\cong{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}}({\mathcal{X}})\cong{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

This is well-known; we briefly recall the construction given in
[
KZ18
]
. Choose
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{B}}
. Certainly we have a braided monoidal embedding
{\mathcal{B}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{B}})
; we just need to show this is essentially surjective. Given an object
b
and enriched half-braiding
\theta
for
b
, notice that enriched naturality means the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[x,y\right]_{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[x\otimes b,y\otimes b\right]_{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[b\otimes x,b\otimes y\right]_{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[b\otimes x,y\otimes b\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\otimes-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{y}}

commutes for all
x,y\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Recall that in a fusion category, internal hom is given by
[x,y]_{\mathcal{B}}\cong x\otimes y^{*}
. Then setting
x=1_{\mathcal{B}}
, we have that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\otimes b\otimes b^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes y\otimes b^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y\otimes b\otimes b^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{by}\circ\eta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}=\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{y}}

commutes for all
y
, so
\theta_{y}=\beta_{b,y}
(where
\beta
is the braiding in
{\mathcal{B}}
). Thus an enriched half-braiding must be the same as the original braiding, so the map
{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{B}})
is essentially surjective and an equivalence. For
{\mathcal{M}}
, choose an indecomposable
{\mathcal{B}}
-module; it follows that
\operatorname{End}({\mathcal{M}}_{\mathcal{B}})\cong{\mathcal{B}}
.
∎

In the arguments that follow, we frequently switch between two different graphical calculi for Gray categories, using the coherence result in
[
GPS95
]
that every 3-category is triequivalent to a Gray category. The first graphical calculus is the 3D graphical calculus found in
[
BMS24
]
. In our graphical calculations, the Deligne tensor product should be read back to front, functor composition read left to right, and natural transformation composition bottom to top. The second graphical calculus is written using string diagrams plus arrows representing transformations of string diagrams. In this graphical calculus, Deligne tensor product is read left to right, functor composition bottom to top, and natural transformation composition is in the time direction. Both of these graphical calculi are coherent for general 3-categories by
[
GPS95
]
,
[
BMS24
]
, and
[
Gut19
]
. We illustrate the two calculi in Figure
1
.

\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}
\scriptstyle F
\scriptstyle G
\scriptstyle\alpha
\scriptstyle\mathcal{E}

\scriptstyle\mathcal{E}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}
\scriptstyle F
\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}

\scriptstyle\mathcal{E}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}
\scriptstyle G
\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Figure 1.
A demonstration of the 3D graphical calculus and 2D string diagram graphical calculus. Both images represent
\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}}}\boxtimes\alpha
as a morphism from
\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{E}}}\boxtimes F\to\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{E}}}\boxtimes G
, where
\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
and
F,G:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
.

In general, we will use brackets to denote homs, with a subscript denoting the enriching category. When the brackets are undecorated, they mean the underlying hom set as a vector space. Sometimes we will refer to “elements” of a hom space
[x,y]
; although this is an object of
{\mathcal{A}}
and not a set, this phrasing is meant to indicate a Yoneda-style generalized elements argument. We spell out the details of any generalized elements argument for enriching categories other than
{\mathcal{A}}
, and choose to elide these arguments for
{\mathcal{A}}
for brevity.

3.
Anchored planar algebras in
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
MB

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be an arbitrary braided pivotal tensor category and
{\mathcal{Y}}
a pivotal
{\mathcal{B}}
-module fusion category. By Lemma
2.2
, there exists a braided fusion category
{\mathcal{A}}
, an
{\mathcal{A}}
-enriched fusion category
{\mathcal{X}}
, and an
{\mathcal{X}}
-module category
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
{\mathcal{B}}\cong{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})
and
{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\operatorname{End}^{\mathcal{A}}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
. Moreover, the action of
{\mathcal{B}}
on
{\mathcal{Y}}
is equivalent to the action of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})\cong\operatorname{End}^{\mathcal{A}}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{X}})
on
\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
. Since
{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
is a fusion category, this action can be denoted by a monoidal functor
\Phi:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
which factors through the Drinfeld center of
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

With this setup, the graphical calculus of strings on tubes for
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
as discussed in
[
HPT15
]
can be thought of as occurring in the graphical calculus for 3-categories in
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
. To clarify our notation, we will use
\boxtimes
for the (relative) Deligne product of module categories,
\otimes
for functor composition, and juxtaposition or
\circ
for natural transformation composition (written in composition order). Some diagrams use the 3-dimensional graphical calculus in
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}
, while others project onto a 2-dimensional graphical calculus of string diagrams with morphisms between them.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be denoted by a sheet. In this context, objects of
{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
can be represented by strings on the sheet. The action of
{\mathcal{Y}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
is given by the endofunctor, while the action of
{\mathcal{Y}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
is given by its adjoint.

3.1.
Construction of an anchored planar algebra in
{\mathcal{A}}-\mathsf{FusCat}

As all the categories involved are finite semisimple, the action functor
-\rhd m:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
has a right adjoint, denoted
[m,-]_{\mathcal{X}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
. This assembles into an enriched internal hom
[-,-]_{\mathcal{X}}:{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
. On objects, this is given by

[m,n]_{\mathcal{X}}:=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{X}})}[x\rhd m,n]\otimes x.

Moreover, this has a natural structure as an
{\mathcal{A}}
-balanced functor; notice that

\displaystyle[m^{\operatorname{op}}\lhd a,n]_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=[(a^{*}\rhd m)^{\operatorname{op}},n]_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{X}})}[x\rhd(a^{*}\rhd m),n]\otimes x

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{X}})}[a^{*}\rhd(x\rhd m),n]\otimes x\cong\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{X}})}[x\rhd m,a\rhd n]\otimes x\cong[m,a\rhd n]_{\mathcal{X}}

Therefore, by the universal property, this induces a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-,-]_{\mathcal{X}}}

The induced functor from
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}
to
{\mathcal{X}}
has a left adjoint by semisimplicity. We now describe this left adjoint explicitly. Using the ladder category model for relative Deligne tensor product in
[
BBJ19
]
, we can think of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}
as the Cauchy completion of the category with objects given by
s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t
for
s,t
simple in
{\mathcal{M}}
, where homs are

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}}(s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t,u^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes v)=\bigoplus_{a\text{ simple}\in{\mathcal{A}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}}(s^{\operatorname{op}},u^{\operatorname{op}}\lhd a)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(a\rhd t,v);

composition is given by stacking ladders.

Consider the object
\bigoplus_{s\in\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{M}})}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s
in
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{M}}
. Choose a basis
\beta^{i}_{a,s,t}
for
[a\rhd s,t]
and corresponding dual basis
(\beta^{i}_{a,s,t})^{*}
for
[t,a\rhd s]
. Then the map

\bigoplus_{a,s,t}\sum_{i}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to39.6pt{\vbox to37.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.99678pt\lower-10.02902pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

lying in
\bigoplus_{s,t}\bigoplus_{a}[a^{*}\rhd t,s]\otimes[a\rhd s,t]\cong\bigoplus_{s,t}\bigoplus_{a}[s^{\operatorname{op}},t^{\operatorname{op}}\lhd a]\otimes[a\rhd s,t]
(where the red vertices represent corresponding elements of the dual bases
\beta_{a,s,t}^{i}
and
(\beta_{a,s,t}^{i})^{*}
), defines an endomorphism
e
of
\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s
. A routine calculation verifies that
e
is an idempotent. Denote by
S
the splitting of the idempotent
e
in the relative Deligne category
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

A unitary version of the object
S
appears in
[
JNP25
, Remark 4.18]
, where it is called the
enriched symmetric enveloping algebra object
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The unique
({\mathcal{X}}-{\mathcal{X}})
-bimodule functor
F:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}
given by
1_{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto S
is left adjoint to the enriched inner hom functor
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
. In fact, these functors are biadjoint.

Proof.

We begin by describing morphisms
f:\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s\to m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n
satisfying
f\circ e=f
(for arbitrary
m,n\in{\mathcal{M}}
), since these correspond to maps out of
S
. Graphically, we denote

where the red and blue trivalent vertices correspond to
\beta^{k}_{b,t,s}
and to corresponding choices of bases
\gamma_{a,b,c}^{j}:a\otimes b\to c
and
(\gamma_{a,b,c}^{j})^{*}:c\to a\otimes b
, respectively. The variables
a,b,c
range over
\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{A}})
, and
s,t
range over
\operatorname{Irr}({\mathcal{M}})
.

We can now use an I=H relation in the multifusion category
\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{X}}&{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\\
{\mathcal{M}}&{\mathcal{A}}\end{bmatrix}
(as in
[
HP17
, Lemma 2.16]
) on the dual bases
\beta^{k}_{b,t,s},\gamma^{j}_{a,b,c}
to obtain

We now use this to describe a map
[1_{\mathcal{X}},[m,n]_{\mathcal{X}}]\cong[m,n]\to[S,m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n]
. It is enough to describe the map in this case by the universal property of Cauchy completion, since
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{M}}
is the Cauchy completion on the subcategory consisting of objects of the form
m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n
. In fact, it is enough to just consider the case where
m,n
are simple. As discussed above, a map
f\in[\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s,m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n]
satisfies
f=f\circ e
, which is true if and only if
f
is in the form

\lambda\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to39.22pt{\vbox to37.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.61484pt\lower-10.02902pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

for some scalar
\lambda
. If
m=n
, then we define the map
[m,m]\to[S,m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes m]
by sending
\lambda\operatorname{id}_{m}
to the map described above. This extends naturally to the rest of the objects in
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes_{{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{M}}
. Moreover, it is clear that this map is an isomorphism, since the space of maps
f\in[\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s,m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n]
satisfying
f=f\circ e
is one dimensional if
m=n
and zero dimensional otherwise. This verifies the adjunction. As left adjoints of linear functors between finite semisimple categories are also right adjoints, these two functors are indeed biadjoint.
∎

where
\eta^{\ell}
and
\epsilon^{\ell}
witness the adjunction
F\dashv F^{*}
and
\eta^{r}
and
\epsilon^{r}
witness
F^{*}\dashv F
.

It is straightforward to verify that the components of these natural transformations are given by

(1)

\eta^{\ell}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}=1_{\mathcal{X}}\xrightarrow{\bigoplus\operatorname{id}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{X}}}\bigoplus_{s}[s,s]\otimes 1_{\mathcal{X}}\hookrightarrow\bigoplus_{s,x}[x\rhd s,s]\otimes x\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{s}[s,s]_{{\mathcal{X}}}\xrightarrow{e}S,

(2)

\epsilon^{\ell}_{t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t^{\prime}}=[t,t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{X}}\rhd S\hookrightarrow\bigoplus_{x,s}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[x\rhd t,t^{\prime}]x\rhd s\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{x}t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[x\rhd t,t^{\prime}]x\rhd t\xrightarrow{\ev}t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t^{\prime},

(3)

\eta^{r}_{t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t^{\prime}}=t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t^{\prime}\cong\bigoplus_{s}[t,s]s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[s,t^{\prime}]s\xrightarrow{\ev}\bigoplus_{s}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[t,t^{\prime}]s\hookrightarrow\bigoplus_{x,s}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[t,t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{X}}\rhd s\xrightarrow{[t,t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{X}}\rhd e}[t,t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{X}}\rhd S,

(4)

\epsilon^{r}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}=S\hookrightarrow\bigoplus_{s}[s,s]_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\bigoplus_{s,x}[x\rhd s,s]\otimes x\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{s}[s,s]\otimes 1_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\bigoplus_{s}1_{\mathcal{X}}\xrightarrow{\sum\operatorname{id}}1_{\mathcal{X}},

where
t,t^{\prime}
are simples in
{\mathcal{M}}
and
x
is a simple in
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Not only are
F
and
F^{*}
biadjoint to each other, but also they witness the duality between
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
G:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
,
G^{*}:{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
be as
F,F^{*}
(replacing
{\mathcal{M}}
with
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
). Then
F
and
G^{*}
witness the duality
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\cong{\mathcal{M}}^{*}
and
G
and
F^{*}
witness the duality
{\mathcal{M}}\cong({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}})^{*}
.

Proof.

To show that
F
and
G^{*}
witness
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\cong{\mathcal{M}}^{*}
, we must demonstrate the existence of cusp isomorphisms
C:(G^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes F)\to\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
D:(\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}}\boxtimes G^{*})\circ(F\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}})\to\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}}
and show they satisfy the swallowtail equations. The other duality is similar and omitted.

By definition of
F
and
G
, the component
C_{m}
must be an isomorphism from
m
to the subjobject of
\bigoplus_{s}[s,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s
given by
(G^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}})(\operatorname{id}_{m}\boxtimes e)
(where
e
is the idempotent in the definition of
S
). Explicitly, this map is given by

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{s}[s,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a,s,t}\sum_{i}[\ev_{a}\rhd s\circ\beta_{a,s,t}^{i},m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s}\bigoplus_{a,s,t}[a^{*}\rhd t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a,s,t}[t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes a\rhd s\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a,s}\sum_{i}\beta_{a,s,t}^{i}}\bigoplus_{t}[t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd t.

Notice that the isomorphism
[a^{*}\rhd t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\cong[t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes a
is

\displaystyle[a^{*}\rhd t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{b}[b\rhd a^{*}\rhd t,m]\otimes b\cong\bigoplus_{b}[a^{*}\rhd t,b^{*}\rhd m]\otimes b\cong\bigoplus_{b,c}[a^{*},b^{*}\otimes c]\otimes[c\rhd t,m]\otimes b

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{b,c}[b,c\otimes a]\otimes[c\rhd t,m]\otimes b\cong\bigoplus_{c}[c\rhd t,m]\otimes c\otimes a\cong[t,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes a.

Unpacking this, we can describe the idempotent in terms of generalized elements (i.e., in terms of maps
x\to\bigoplus_{s}[s,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s=\bigoplus_{s,a}[a\rhd s,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes a\rhd s
). This requires describing the action of the idempotent on the vector space
\bigoplus_{a,s}[a\rhd s,m]\otimes[x,a\rhd s]
. This map is given by:

\bigoplus_{a,s}\sum_{i}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to29.22pt{\vbox to38.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.76974pt\lower-7.68523pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

with the green and purple circles representing
\delta_{c,t,r}^{j}
and
\epsilon_{r,a,s}^{k}
, another pair of bases and dual bases.

We now show that
m
is equivalent to the subobject generated by this idempotent. It is enough to do this in the case where
m
is simple.
Consider the map
i:m\to\bigoplus_{s}[s,m]_{\mathcal{A}}\rhd s\cong\bigoplus_{a,s}[a\rhd s,m]\otimes a\rhd s

by
\bigoplus_{a,s}\sum_{i}\beta_{a,s,m}^{i}\otimes(\beta_{a,s,m}^{i})^{*}
.
It is immediately apparent that this map and its dual split the idempotent above, exhibiting
m
as the subobject of this isomorphism. This defines the component of
C
at the object
m
; the isomorphism
D
is formally dual.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

The natural map

\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}\xRightarrow{\eta^{\ell}_{G}}(G^{*}\circ G)\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}\xRightarrow{\eta_{F}^{r}}(F^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes G)\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes G^{*})\circ(F\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}})\xRightarrow{C\circ C}\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}

is the identity on
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

By duality, there are similar versions of Lemma
3.4
for
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
and for the composite
(C\circ C)\eta_{G}^{r}\eta_{F}^{\ell}
.

We denote the map
y\mapsto F^{*}\circ({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)\circ F
by
\operatorname{Tr}:\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})
; this corresponds to the categorified trace of
[
HPT15
]
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The map
\operatorname{Tr}:\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})
given by
F^{*}\circ({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes-)\circ F
is right adjoint to the action map
\Phi:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})\to\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
[1_{\mathcal{X}},F^{*}yF]\cong[\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}},y]
for all
y:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
since all the functors involved are module functors. By Proposition
3.2
, we have

[1_{\mathcal{X}},F^{*}yF]\cong[S,yF]=[S,({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(S)],

which is isomorphic to the image of
[S,({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s)]
after cutting down by the idempotent
({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(e)
. Now
({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s)=\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes ys\cong\bigoplus_{s,t}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{y,s}^{t}}\otimes t
, where
n_{y,s}^{t}
denotes the dimension of
[t,ys]
. Let
n=\max(n_{y,s}^{t})
; then this object includes into
\left(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t\right)^{\oplus n}
. Recalling the definition of
e
, it follows that the image of the idempotent
({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(e)
is the same as the image of the map

\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes ys\hookrightarrow\left(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t\right)^{\oplus n}\twoheadrightarrow\left(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s\right)^{\oplus n}\twoheadrightarrow S^{\oplus n}.

If we are mapping out of
S
, though, we are already mapping into this copy of
S^{\oplus n}
. Thus
[S,({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(S)]\cong[S,({\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes y)(\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s)]
. Finally, notice that we have
[S,\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes ys]\cong\bigoplus[s,ys]\cong[\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{M}},y]
by Proposition
3.2
and semisimplicity. ∎

Lemma 3.6
.

The unit of the adjunction
\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}
is given by tensoring with
\eta^{\ell}

Both of these are equal to the identity by an application of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

This multiplication agrees with the multiplication map given in
[
HPT15
]
.

Proposition 3.7
.

The map
\mu_{x,y}
is the mate of the map

\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x)\circ\operatorname{Tr}(y))\xrightarrow{\mu_{\Phi}}\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x))\circ\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(y))\xrightarrow{\epsilon\circ\epsilon}x\circ y

under the adjunction
\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}
.

Proof.

We want to show that
\mu_{x,y}
is equal to the map

\operatorname{Tr}(x)\circ\operatorname{Tr}(y)\Rightarrow\operatorname{Tr}(\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x)\circ\operatorname{Tr}(y)))\cong\operatorname{Tr}(\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x))\circ\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(y)))\Rightarrow\operatorname{Tr}(x\circ y).

From the characterization of the unit and counit of
\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}
in Lemma
3.6
, we can write this map in the graphical calculus of string diagrams as

Most of the cells are naturality squares. The upper left triangle commutes by the snake for
\eta^{\ell}_{G},\epsilon^{\ell}_{G}
, and the lower left triangle commutes by an application of Lemma
3.4
. The upper map is the mate of
\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x)\circ\operatorname{Tr}(y))\xrightarrow{\mu_{\Phi}}\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(x))\circ\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(y))\xrightarrow{\epsilon\circ\epsilon}x\circ y
, as described above, while the lower map is
\mu_{x,y}
. Thus the two multiplications are equal.
∎

This multiplication also agrees with the two above (under the interchanger
\phi:\operatorname{Tr}(x)\boxtimes\operatorname{Tr}(y)\cong\operatorname{Tr}(x)\circ\operatorname{Tr}(y)
), via a similar proof to above. Lemma
3.4
records the fact that this multiplication similarly absorbs the unit
\eta^{\ell}
.

Since
\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}
, we have canonical traciators, as shown in
[
HPT15
]
. In this context, these traciators can be broken down as the composition of two half twists, denoted graphically by

where the red string denotes a module functor
y:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
. The components of these natural transformations are given by

(5)

(\tau^{\ell}_{y})_{1}=e\left(\bigoplus_{s\in{\mathcal{M}}}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes ys\cong\bigoplus_{s,t}s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes[t,ys]\otimes t\cong\bigoplus_{s,t}s^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes[y^{*}t,s]\boxtimes t\cong\bigoplus_{t}y^{*}t^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t\right)e,

and

(6)

(\tau^{r}_{y})_{m^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes n}=\Bigg([m,yn]_{{\mathcal{X}}}\cong\bigoplus_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}[x\rhd m,yn]\otimes x\cong\bigoplus_{x}[y^{*}(x\rhd m),n]\otimes x\\
\cong\bigoplus_{x}[x\rhd(y^{*}m),n]\otimes x\cong[y^{*}m,n]_{\mathcal{X}}\Bigg).

It is immediate that these are both natural isomorphisms. They also satisfy compatibilities with the structures defined above.

Proof.

This is equivalent to showing

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to18.59pt{\vbox to41.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.31406pt\lower-8.7359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -6.52 5.29 -11.81 11.81 -11.81 C 18.33 -11.81 23.62 -6.52 23.62 0 L 23.62 39.37 C 23.62 42.63 18.33 45.28 11.81 45.28 C 5.29 45.28 0 42.63 0 39.37 C 0 36.11 5.29 33.46 11.81 33.46 C 18.33 33.46 23.62 36.11 23.62 39.37 C 23.62 42.63 18.33 45.28 11.81 45.28 C 5.29 45.28 0 42.63 0 39.37 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\,.

It is enough to show that their components at
1_{\mathcal{X}}
are equal, since both are bimodule natural transformations. Moreover, since
\eta_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}^{\ell}
is defined as a map into
\bigoplus s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes s
followed by the idempotent
e
, it is sufficient to compute this equality on maps into this larger object, and then project into the subobject
S
.

We will show the Yoneda embeddings of these components are equal, i.e., that the induced maps
[x,1_{\mathcal{X}}]\to[x,\bigoplus[s,yy^{*}s]_{\mathcal{X}}]
are equal for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Recall that by (
1
), the component
\eta^{\ell}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}:1_{\mathcal{X}}\to\bigoplus[s,s]_{\mathcal{X}}
corestricts to the summand at
1_{\mathcal{X}}
. Since we are precomposing with
\eta^{\ell}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}
, we only need to consider the case
x=1_{\mathcal{X}}
, as all the other induced maps will be zero.

The map on the left is just

1_{\mathcal{X}}\xrightarrow{\eta^{\ell}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}}\bigoplus[s,s]_{\mathcal{X}}\xrightarrow{[\operatorname{id},\coev]}\bigoplus[s,yy^{*}x]_{\mathcal{X}},

which is represented at
1_{\mathcal{X}}
by

\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{s}\operatorname{id}_{s}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{s}\coev^{y}_{s}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}.

For the other map, we begin by calculating the components of the traciators, when mapped into by
1_{\mathcal{X}}
. First, recall that the component at
s^{\operatorname{op}}\boxtimes t
of
\tau^{r}
is given by

[s,yt]_{\mathcal{X}}=\bigoplus_{x}[x\lhd s,yt]\otimes x\cong\bigoplus_{x}[y^{*}(x\lhd s),t]\otimes x\cong\bigoplus_{x}[x\lhd y^{*}s,t]\otimes x=[y^{*}s,t]_{\mathcal{X}}.

This acts on maps from
1_{\mathcal{X}}
into this object by sending elements of
[s,yt]
to elements of
[y^{*}s,t]
via the adjunction, that is, by composing with the evaluation of the
y^{*},y
adjunction. Since the map in the graphical image above is actually
(\tau^{r})^{-1}
, it is given by composing with the coevaluation of the adjunction. Similarly, for
\tau^{\ell}
, since we are restricting to the component at
1_{\mathcal{X}}
, we can again notice by (
5
) that the map is given by composing with the coevaluation.

Finally, notice that the cup on the back of the tube is actually
\ev^{\operatorname{op}}
, since left and right duals are switched in
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Thus the map on the right is represented at
1_{\mathcal{X}}
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{s}\operatorname{id}_{s}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigoplus_{s}(\ev^{y}_{s})^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigoplus(\operatorname{id}_{y}\otimes\ev^{y}_{s}\operatorname{id}_{y^{*}s})\circ(\coev^{y}_{s}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{yy^{*}s})\circ(\coev^{y}_{s})\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus\coev^{y}_{s}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{1_{\mathcal{X}}},

where the last equality is due to the snake equation for
y
.
∎

We can compose the half traciators
\tau_{x}^{\ell}
and
(\tau_{x}^{r})^{-1}
, represented graphically by

to define a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}(x\otimes y)\to\operatorname{Tr}(y\otimes x)
(where
x
denotes the red string).

Proposition 3.10
.

The composition of
\tau_{x}^{\ell}
and
\tau_{x}^{r}
defines a traciator; that is, for
x,y\in\operatorname{End}({}_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
, the map
\tau_{x,y}:\operatorname{Tr}(x\otimes y)\to\operatorname{Tr}(y\otimes x)
represented above satisfies the condition
\tau_{x\otimes y,z}=\tau_{x,y\otimes z}\circ\tau_{y,x\otimes z}
as in
[
HPT15
]
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\tau^{\ell}_{x\otimes y}=\tau^{\ell}_{y}\circ\tau^{\ell}_{x}
and
\tau^{r}_{x\otimes y}=\tau^{r}_{x}\circ\tau^{r}_{y}
. For
\tau^{\ell}
, this follows from the definition in (
5
) and the fact that the isomorphism in the adjunction
[t,xys]\cong[(xy)^{*}t,s]
is the same as the composite
[t,xys]\cong[x^{*}t,ys]\cong[y^{*}x^{*}t,s]\cong[(xy)^{*}t,s]
. Similarly, for
\tau^{r}
, this follows from the compatibility of the
{\mathcal{X}}
-module structures of
x
and
y
with composition of
x
and
y
and the compatibility of composition with the adjunction data. The result follows.
∎

We now prove the claim above, that the traciator given by composing the two half-traciators is the same as the natural one arising from the adjunction
\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

The traciator in Proposition
3.10
is the same as the one given in
[
HPT15
]
.

Proof.

The traciator in
[
HPT15
]
is given as the mate of the map

\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(xy))\xrightarrow{\coev_{y}}\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(xy))yy^{*}\xrightarrow{\beta}y\Phi(\operatorname{Tr}(xy))y^{*}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{\Phi\dashv\operatorname{Tr}}}yxyy^{*}\xrightarrow{p}yxy^{**}y^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev_{y^{*}}}yx.

(Here,

represents the interchanger). Most of the faces in this diagram are naturality squares or snake equations. The red squares are both applications of Lemma
3.9
, and the blue triangle is an application of Proposition
3.7
. Therefore, the two paths around the outside of the diagram are equal. One is the traciator defined in

, while the other is
\tau_{\ell}\circ\tau_{r}

∎

As a result of Proposition
3.11
, we obtain coherence results for the traciators and the braiding and twist isomorphisms, as in
[
HPT15
]
. However, we provide alternate proofs below using the graphical calculus within the 3-category. This demonstrates the usefulness of the 3-categorical approach.

.

To verify these are equal, consider the following pasting diagram:

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{r}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Here the map labeled
\mu
is the multiplication mentioned in Remark
3.8
; the triangle and square containing
\mu
commute by the remark. The other faces are naturality squares or trivial, so this completes the proof.
∎

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 35.43 L 47.24 66.93}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=\theta_{\operatorname{Tr}(y)},

where
\theta
is the natural balancing arising from the pivotality and braiding, and
y
is the red string.

Proposition 3.13
.

The traciator given in Proposition
3.10
satisfies Equation
8
.

Proof.

Recall that the balancing arising from a pivotal braided category is given by
(\ev_{x}\otimes p_{x^{**}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{x}\otimes\beta)\circ(\operatorname{id}_{x}\otimes\coev_{x^{*}})
, where
p
is the pivotality isomorphism
x^{**}\cong x
. As
\operatorname{Tr}(y)
is a composite of three functors, its evaluation and coevaluation maps are given by composites of the evaluation and coevaluations for its parts. In the following diagram, this composite goes around the top and right edges.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coev}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev^{\operatorname{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau^{r})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau^{r})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\ell}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{r}}

Here, the red squares commute by (variants of) Lemma
3.9
, and the blue polygon commutes by Proposition
3.12
. The map around the top is the twist automorphism, as discussed above, while the map around the left and bottom is the trace map on the left side of (
8
).
∎

3.2.
Equivalence with anchored planar algebras

In
[
HPT16
]
, it is shown that there is a correspondence between anchored planar algebras in a braided pivotal category
{\mathcal{B}}
and pointed pivotal module tensor categories over
{\mathcal{B}}
. The construction above clearly corresponds to a pointed pivotal module tensor category: choose any self-dual
y\in\operatorname{End}({\mathcal{M}})
, and take the pointed pivotal module tensor subcategory generated by this object. Moreover, it is clear that any pointed pivotal module tensor category with a self-dual generator can arise this way by Lemma
2.2
. The content of Propositions
3.7
and
3.11
shows that the generating maps agree between these two constructions. It remains to be seen that the above construction defines an anchored planar algebra.

Theorem 3.14

(Theorem
A
)
.

Suppose we have a planar pivotal 3-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, objects
A,B
, morphisms
M:A\to B
and
M^{*}:B\to A
, and adjoint pair
F:M\boxtimes_{B}M^{*}\to\operatorname{id}_{A}
,
F^{*}:\operatorname{id}_{A}\to M\boxtimes_{B}M^{*}
with isomorphisms

\tau^{\ell}:(\operatorname{id}_{M}\boxtimes-)\circ F\to(-^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{M}^{*})\circ F,\ \ \tau^{r}:F^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id}_{M}\boxtimes-)\to F^{*}\circ(-^{*}\boxtimes\operatorname{id}_{M}^{*}),

satisfying the compatibilities in
equations (
7
) and (
8
). Then for every self-dual endomorphism
x
in
\operatorname{End}(M)
, we can construct an anchored planar algebra internal to
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{id}_{A})
which interprets anchored planar tangles via the graphical calculus of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

In
[
HPT16
]
, the authors provide a small list of generators and relations that are equivalent to those for an anchored planar algebra. Given a braided pivotal category
{\mathcal{B}}
, an anchored planar algebra inside
{\mathcal{B}}
is the same as a list of objects
{\mathcal{P}}[i]\in{\mathcal{B}}
for each
i
and generators

\eta:1\to{\mathcal{P}}[0]\qquad\alpha_{i}:{\mathcal{P}}[n+2]\to{\mathcal{P}}[n]\qquad\tilde{\alpha}_{i}:{\mathcal{P}}[n]\to{\mathcal{P}}[n+2]\qquad\omega_{i,j}:{\mathcal{P}}[n]\otimes{\mathcal{P}}[j]\to{\mathcal{P}}[n+j]

satisfying a list of 9 axioms (C1)
-
(C9).
As
{\mathcal{C}}
is a planar pivotal 3-category,
\operatorname{End}(\operatorname{id}_{A})
is a braided pivotal category. We choose
{\mathcal{P}}[i]=F\circ x^{n}\circ F^{*}
. The generator
\eta
is given by the unit of the adjunction. The generators
\alpha_{i}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
are the cup and cap for
x
(as
x
is self-dual), evaluated at the
i
th position. The generator
\omega_{i,j}
is given graphically by the diagram

Now many of the axioms follow immediately. Axiom (C1) states that
\eta
is a unit for the multiplication
\omega_{0,j}
and
\omega_{i,0}
, which follows immediately from the snake equations for the adjunction between
F
and
F^{*}
. Axioms (C2), (C3), (C5), and (C6) follow from naturality of cups and caps for
x
and naturality of the half-traciators. Axiom (C4) follows from naturality of cups and caps and the snake equations. Finally, axiom (C7) is an associativity condition for the multiplication, (C8) is the compatibility with the braiding, and (C9) is the compatibility with the twist. The associativity condition is immediate by properties of adjunctions, and (C8) and (C9) follow from (
7
) and (
8
), respectively. Thus we obtain an anchored planar algebra, as claimed.
∎

Theorem 3.15

(Theorem
B
)
.

All anchored planar algebras internal to braided fusion categories can be obtained via the construction in Theorem
3.14
.

Proof.

Given an anchored planar algebra
{\mathcal{P}}
in
{\mathcal{B}}\cong\operatorname{End}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{X}})
, by
[
HPT16
]
it corresponds to some pointed pivotal module tensor category
{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\operatorname{End}^{\mathcal{A}}(_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{M}})
. Following the construction in Section
3.1
, we obtain maps satisfying the conditions in Theorem
3.14
, and thus an anchored planar algebra. By Propositions
3.7
and
3.11
, the generating maps of this anchored planar algebra agree with the generating maps of the construction in
[
HPT16
]
, and so this anchored planar algebra is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

3.3.
Application to rigidity

The advantage of this construction is that it greatly expands the graphical language of anchored planar algebras. The shapes defined in Section
3.1
can be used to build and interpret surfaces of higher genus, extending the anchored planar operad. Additionally, abstract categorical tools can be applied to constructions in any anchored planar algebra.

One question this approach answers immediately has to do with the duality pairing of objects of the form
\operatorname{Tr}(y)
. In
[
HPT24
]
, it was shown that the multiplication gives a duality pairing in the unitary setting, but the general question asked in
[
HPT15
, Remark 5.4]
was left open. Here, we can answer it.

Corollary 3.16
.

The rigidity of
\operatorname{Tr}(y)
is witnessed by the adjunction data above; that is,

{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -11.81 C 23.62 27.33 50.06 59.06 82.68 59.06 C 115.29 59.06 141.73 27.33 141.73 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

is a non-degenerate pairing between
\operatorname{Tr}(y)
and
\operatorname{Tr}(y^{*})
.

Proof.

The adjoint of a composite is the composite of adjoints, with witnesses being the composite of the witnesses for the adjunction, so this follows from Proposition
3.2
. Pictorially, the composite on the left decomposes as

F

\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}}\boxtimes y

F^{*}

F

\operatorname{id}_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{op}}}\boxtimes y

F^{*}

and notice that the three central maps stacked in the middle are all evaluations.
∎
